</div><div class="mw-content-ltr mw-parser-output" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Scientific study of language</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1320445320">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}@media print{body.ns-0 .mw-parser-output .hatnote{display:none!important}}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the field of study. For publications, see <a href="/wiki/Linguistics_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Linguistics (disambiguation)">Linguistics (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Linguist" redirects here. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Linguist_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Linguist (disambiguation)">Linguist (disambiguation)</a>.</div>

print{.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:none!important}}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Linguistics" title="Template:Linguistics"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Linguistics" title="Template talk:Linguistics"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Template:Linguistics" title="Special:EditPage/Template:Linguistics"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Linguistics</b> is the scientific study of <a href="/wiki/Language" title="Language">language</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>1<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>2<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>3<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The areas of linguistic analysis are <a href="/wiki/Syntax" title="Syntax">syntax</a> (rules governing the structure of sentences), <a href="/wiki/Semantics" title="Semantics">semantics</a> (meaning), <a href="/wiki/Morphology_(linguistics)" title="Morphology (linguistics)">morphology</a> (structure of words), <a href="/wiki/Phonetics" title="Phonetics">phonetics</a> (speech sounds and equivalent gestures in <a href="/wiki/Sign_language" title="Sign language">sign languages</a>), <a href="/wiki/Phonology" title="Phonology">phonology</a> (the abstract sound system of a particular language, and analogous systems of sign languages), and <a href="/wiki/Pragmatics" title="Pragmatics">pragmatics</a> (how the context of use contributes to meaning).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-akmajian&#95;4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-akmajian-4"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>4<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Subdisciplines such as <a href="/wiki/Biolinguistics" title="Biolinguistics">biolinguistics</a> (the study of the biological variables and evolution of language) and <a href="/wiki/Psycholinguistics" title="Psycholinguistics">psycholinguistics</a> (the study of psychological factors in human language) bridge many of these divisions.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>5<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Linguistics encompasses <a href="/wiki/Outline_of_linguistics" title="Outline of linguistics">many branches and subfields</a> that span both theoretical and practical applications.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-linguisticsociety.org&#95;6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-linguisticsociety.org-6"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>6<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Theoretical_linguistics" title="Theoretical linguistics">Theoretical linguistics</a> is concerned with understanding the <a href="/wiki/Universal_grammar" title="Universal grammar">universal</a> and <a href="/wiki/Philosophy_of_language#Nature_of_language" title="Philosophy of language">fundamental nature</a> of language and developing a general theoretical framework for describing it. <a href="/wiki/Applied_linguistics" title="Applied linguistics">Applied linguistics</a> seeks to utilize the scientific findings of the study of language for practical purposes, such as developing methods of improving language education and literacy. <a href="/wiki/Mathematical_linguistics" title="Mathematical linguistics">Mathematical linguistics</a> is the application of <a href="/wiki/Mathematics" title="Mathematics">mathematics</a> to model phenomena and solve problems in general linguistics and <a href="/wiki/Theoretical_linguistics" title="Theoretical linguistics">theoretical linguistics</a>. <a href="/wiki/Computational_linguistics" title="Computational linguistics">Computational linguistics</a> is an <a href="/wiki/Interdisciplinarity" title="Interdisciplinarity">interdisciplinary</a> field concerned with the <a href="/wiki/Computational_modelling" class="mw-redirect" title="Computational modelling">computational modelling</a> of <a href="/wiki/Natural_language" title="Natural language">natural language</a>, as well as the study of appropriate computational approaches to linguistic questions. 
</p><p>Linguistic features may be studied through a variety of perspectives: <a href="/wiki/Synchronic_analysis" class="mw-redirect" title="Synchronic analysis">synchronically</a> (by describing the structure of a language at a specific point in time) or <a href="/wiki/Historical_linguistics" title="Historical linguistics">diachronically</a> (through the historical development of a language over a period of time), in <a href="/wiki/Monolingualism" title="Monolingualism">monolinguals</a> or in <a href="/wiki/Multilingualism" title="Multilingualism">multilinguals</a>, among children or among adults, in terms of how it is being learnt or how it was acquired, as abstract objects or as cognitive structures, through written texts or through oral elicitation, and finally through mechanical data collection or practical fieldwork.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>7<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Linguistics emerged from the field of <a href="/wiki/Philology" title="Philology">philology</a>, of which some branches are more qualitative and <a href="/wiki/Holistic" class="mw-redirect" title="Holistic">holistic</a> in approach.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Crystal-1981&#95;8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Crystal-1981-8"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>8<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Today, philology and linguistics are variably described as related fields, subdisciplines, or separate fields of language study, but, by and large, linguistics can be seen as an umbrella term.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>9<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Linguistics is also related to the <a href="/wiki/Philosophy_of_language" title="Philosophy of language">philosophy of language</a>, <a href="/wiki/Stylistics" title="Stylistics">stylistics</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rhetoric" title="Rhetoric">rhetoric</a>, <a href="/wiki/Semiotics" title="Semiotics">semiotics</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lexicography" title="Lexicography">lexicography</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Translation" title="Translation">translation</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Major_subdisciplines">Major subdisciplines</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Major subdisciplines"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Citation_de_Ferdinand_de_Saussure.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Citation_de_Ferdinand_de_Saussure.jpg/250px-Citation_de_Ferdinand_de_Saussure.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Citation_de_Ferdinand_de_Saussure.jpg/500px-Citation_de_Ferdinand_de_Saussure.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="6000" data-file-height="4000" /></a><figcaption>Swiss linguist <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_de_Saussure" title="Ferdinand de Saussure">Ferdinand de Saussure</a> is regarded as the creator of <a href="/wiki/Semiotics" title="Semiotics">semiotics</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Historical_linguistics">Historical linguistics</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Historical linguistics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Historical_linguistics" title="Historical linguistics">Historical linguistics</a></div>
<p>Historical linguistics is the study of how language changes over history, particularly with regard to a specific language or a group of languages. <a href="/wiki/Western_world" title="Western world">Western trends</a> in historical linguistics date back to roughly the late 18th century, when the discipline grew out of <a href="/wiki/Philology" title="Philology">philology</a>, the study of ancient texts and oral traditions.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>10<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Historical linguistics emerged as one of the first few sub-disciplines in the field, and was most widely practised during the late 19th century.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>11<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Despite a shift in focus in the 20th century towards <a href="/wiki/Formalism_(linguistics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Formalism (linguistics)">formalism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Generative_grammar" title="Generative grammar">generative grammar</a>, which studies the <a href="/wiki/Universal_grammar" title="Universal grammar">universal</a> properties of language, historical research today still remains a significant field of linguistic inquiry. Subfields of the discipline include <a href="/wiki/Language_change" title="Language change">language change</a> and <a href="/wiki/Grammaticalization" title="Grammaticalization">grammaticalization</a>.
</p><p>Historical linguistics studies language change either diachronically (through a comparison of different time periods in the past and present) or in a <a href="/wiki/Synchronic_linguistics" class="mw-redirect" title="Synchronic linguistics">synchronic</a> manner (by observing developments between different variations that exist within the current linguistic stage of a language).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>12<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>At first, historical linguistics was the cornerstone of <a href="/wiki/Comparative_linguistics" title="Comparative linguistics">comparative linguistics</a>, which involves a study of the relationship between different languages.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Routledge&#95;introduction&#95;13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Routledge_introduction-13"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>13<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> At that time, scholars of historical linguistics were only concerned with creating different categories of <a href="/wiki/Language_family" title="Language family">language families</a>, and reconstructing <a href="/wiki/Prehistory" title="Prehistory">prehistoric</a> proto-languages by using both the <a href="/wiki/Comparative_method" title="Comparative method">comparative method</a> and the method of <a href="/wiki/Internal_reconstruction" title="Internal reconstruction">internal reconstruction</a>. Internal reconstruction is the method by which an element that contains a certain meaning is re-used in different contexts or environments where there is a variation in either sound or analogy.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Routledge&#95;introduction&#95;13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Routledge_introduction-13"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>13<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint noexcerpt Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Questionable_sources" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="This claim needs references to better sources. (December 2020)">better&#160;source&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The reason for this had been to describe well-known <a href="/wiki/Indo-European_languages" title="Indo-European languages">Indo-European languages</a>, many of which had detailed documentation and long written histories. Scholars of historical linguistics also studied <a href="/wiki/Uralic_languages" title="Uralic languages">Uralic languages</a>, another European language family for which very little written material existed back then. After that, there also followed significant work on the <a href="/wiki/Text_corpus" title="Text corpus">corpora</a> of other languages, such as the <a href="/wiki/Austronesian_languages" title="Austronesian languages">Austronesian languages</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Native_American_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Native American language">Native American language families</a>.
</p><p>In historical work, the <a href="/wiki/Uniformitarian_Principle_(linguistics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Uniformitarian Principle (linguistics)">uniformitarian principle</a> is generally the underlying working hypothesis, occasionally also clearly expressed.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>14<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The principle was expressed early by <a href="/wiki/William_Dwight_Whitney" title="William Dwight Whitney">William Dwight Whitney</a>, who considered it imperative, a "must", of historical linguistics to "look to find the same principle operative also in the very outset of that [language] history."<sup id="cite&#95;ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>15<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The above approach of comparativism in linguistics is now, however, only a small part of the much broader discipline called historical linguistics. The comparative study of specific Indo-European languages is considered a highly specialized field today, while comparative research is carried out over the subsequent internal developments in a language: in particular, over the development of modern standard varieties of languages, and over the development of a language from its standardized form to its varieties.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2024)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>For instance, some scholars also tried to establish <a href="/wiki/Macrofamily" title="Macrofamily">super-families</a>, linking, for example, Indo-European, Uralic, and other language families to a hypothetical <a href="/wiki/Nostratic_languages" title="Nostratic languages">Nostratic language group</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>16<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Reliable_sources" title="Wikipedia:Reliable sources"><span title="The material near this tag may rely on an unreliable source. (March 2026)">unreliable source?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> These attempts are still not widely accepted as credible methods. A limit of around 10,000 years is often assumed for the functional purpose of conducting research.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>17<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> It is also hard to date various proto-languages. Even though several methods are available, these languages can be dated only approximately.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>18<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In modern historical linguistics, we examine how languages change over time, focusing on the relationships between dialects within a specific period. This includes studying morphological, syntactical, and phonetic shifts. Connections between dialects in the past and present are also explored.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Acadmia&#95;Morphology&#95;19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Acadmia_Morphology-19"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>19<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Syntax">Syntax</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Syntax"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Syntax" title="Syntax">Syntax</a></div>
<p>Syntax is the study of how words and <a href="/wiki/Morpheme" title="Morpheme">morphemes</a> combine to form larger units such as <a href="/wiki/Phrase" title="Phrase">phrases</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sentence_(linguistics)" title="Sentence (linguistics)">sentences</a>. Central concerns of syntax include <a href="/wiki/Word_order" title="Word order">word order</a>, <a href="/wiki/Grammatical_relations" class="mw-redirect" title="Grammatical relations">grammatical relations</a>, <a href="/wiki/Constituent_(linguistics)" title="Constituent (linguistics)">constituency</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>20<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Agreement_(linguistics)" title="Agreement (linguistics)">agreement</a>, the nature of crosslinguistic variation, and the relationship between form and meaning. There are numerous approaches to syntax that differ in their central assumptions and goals.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Morphology">Morphology</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Morphology"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Morphology_(linguistics)" title="Morphology (linguistics)">Morphology (linguistics)</a></div>
<p>Morphology is the study of <a href="/wiki/Word" title="Word">words</a>, including the principles by which they are formed, and how they relate to one another within a language.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>21<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>22<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Most approaches to morphology investigate the structure of words in terms of <i><a href="/wiki/Morpheme" title="Morpheme">morphemes</a></i>, which are the smallest units in a language with some independent <a href="/wiki/Meaning_(linguistics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Meaning (linguistics)">meaning</a>. Morphemes include <a href="/wiki/Root_(linguistics)" title="Root (linguistics)">roots</a> that can exist as words by themselves, but also categories such as <a href="/wiki/Affix" title="Affix">affixes</a> that can only appear as part of a larger word. For example, in English the root <i>catch</i> and the suffix <i>-ing</i> are both morphemes; <i>catch</i> may appear as its own word, or it may be combined with <i>-ing</i> to form the new word <i>catching</i>. Morphology also analyzes how words behave as <a href="/wiki/Parts_of_speech" class="mw-redirect" title="Parts of speech">parts of speech</a>, and how they may be <a href="/wiki/Inflected" class="mw-redirect" title="Inflected">inflected</a> to express <a href="/wiki/Grammatical_categories" class="mw-redirect" title="Grammatical categories">grammatical categories</a> including <a href="/wiki/Grammatical_number" title="Grammatical number">number</a>, <a href="/wiki/Grammatical_tense" title="Grammatical tense">tense</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Grammatical_aspect" title="Grammatical aspect">aspect</a>. Concepts such as <a href="/wiki/Productivity_(linguistics)" title="Productivity (linguistics)">productivity</a> are concerned with how speakers create words in specific contexts, which evolves over the history of a language.
</p><p>The discipline that deals specifically with the sound changes occurring within morphemes is <a href="/wiki/Morphophonology" title="Morphophonology">morphophonology</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Semantics_and_pragmatics">Semantics and pragmatics</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Semantics and pragmatics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Semantics" title="Semantics">Semantics</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pragmatics" title="Pragmatics">Pragmatics</a></div>
<p>Semantics and pragmatics are branches of linguistics concerned with meaning. These subfields have traditionally been divided according to aspects of meaning: "semantics" refers to grammatical and lexical meanings, while "pragmatics" is concerned with meaning in context. Within linguistics, the subfield of <a href="/wiki/Formal_semantics_(natural_language)" title="Formal semantics (natural language)">formal semantics</a> studies the <a href="/wiki/Denotation" title="Denotation">denotations</a> of sentences and how they are <a href="/wiki/Compositionality" class="mw-redirect" title="Compositionality">composed</a> from the meanings of their constituent expressions. Formal semantics draws heavily on <a href="/wiki/Philosophy_of_language" title="Philosophy of language">philosophy of language</a> and uses formal tools from logic and <a href="/wiki/Computer_science" title="Computer science">computer science</a>. On the other hand, <a href="/wiki/Cognitive_semantics" title="Cognitive semantics">cognitive semantics</a> explains linguistic meaning via aspects of general cognition, drawing on ideas from cognitive science such as <a href="/wiki/Prototype_theory" title="Prototype theory">prototype theory</a>.
</p><p>Pragmatics focuses on phenomena such as <a href="/wiki/Speech_act" title="Speech act">speech acts</a>, <a href="/wiki/Implicature" title="Implicature">implicature</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Conversation_analysis" title="Conversation analysis">talk in interaction</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Mey&#95;23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mey-23"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>23<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Unlike semantics, which examines meaning that is conventional or "coded" in a given language, pragmatics studies how the transmission of meaning depends not only on the structural and linguistic knowledge (grammar, lexicon, etc.) of the speaker and listener, but also on the context of the utterance,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>24<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> any pre-existing knowledge about those involved, the inferred intent of the speaker, and other factors.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>25<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Phonetics_and_phonology">Phonetics and phonology</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Phonetics and phonology"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Phonetics" title="Phonetics">Phonetics</a> and <a href="/wiki/Phonology" title="Phonology">Phonology</a></div>
<p>Phonetics and phonology are branches of linguistics concerned with sounds (or the equivalent aspects of sign languages). Phonetics is largely concerned with the physical aspects of sounds such as their <a href="/wiki/Articulatory_phonetics" title="Articulatory phonetics">articulation</a>, acoustics, production, and perception. <a href="/wiki/Phonology" title="Phonology">Phonology</a> is concerned with the linguistic abstractions and categorizations of sounds, and it tells us what sounds are in a language, how they do and can combine into words, and explains why certain phonetic features are important to identifying a word.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>26<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Typology">Typology</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Typology"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<div class="excerpt-block"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1066933788">.mw-parser-output .excerpt-hat .mw-editsection-like{font-style:normal}</style><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable dablink excerpt-hat selfref">This paragraph is an excerpt from <a href="/wiki/Linguistic_typology" title="Linguistic typology">Linguistic typology</a>.<span class="mw-editsection-like plainlinks"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Linguistic_typology&amp;action=edit">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div><div class="excerpt">
<p><a href="/wiki/Linguistic_typology" title="Linguistic typology">Linguistic typology</a> (or language typology) is a field of linguistics that studies and classifies languages according to their structural features to allow their comparison. Its aim is to describe and explain the structural diversity and the common properties of the world's languages.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Linguistic&#95;typology&#95;:12&#95;27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Linguistic_typology_:12-27"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>27<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Its subdisciplines include, but are not limited to phonological typology, which deals with sound features; syntactic typology, which deals with word order and form; lexical typology, which deals with language vocabulary; and theoretical typology, which aims to explain the universal tendencies.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>28<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
</div></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Structures">Structures</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Structures"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1305433154" /><table class="box-Unreferenced&#95;section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Unreferenced" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/60px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/120px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>does not <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources">cite</a> any <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">sources</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Linguistics" title="Special:EditPage/Linguistics">improve this section</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>. Unsourced material may be challenged and <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">removed</a>.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">January 2019</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Linguistic structures are pairings of meaning and form. Any particular pairing of meaning and form is a <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_de_Saussure" title="Ferdinand de Saussure">Saussurean</a> <a href="/wiki/Sign_(semiotics)" title="Sign (semiotics)">linguistic sign</a>. For instance, the meaning "cat" is represented worldwide with a wide variety of different sound patterns (in oral languages), movements of the hands and face (in <a href="/wiki/Sign_language" title="Sign language">sign languages</a>), and written symbols (in written languages). Linguistic patterns have proven their importance for the <a href="/wiki/Knowledge_engineering" title="Knowledge engineering">knowledge engineering</a> field especially with the ever-increasing amount of available data.
</p><p>Linguists focusing on structure attempt to understand the rules regarding language use that native speakers know (not always consciously). All linguistic structures can be broken down into component parts that are combined according to (sub)conscious rules, over multiple levels of analysis. For instance, consider the structure of the word "tenth" on two different levels of analysis. On the level of internal word structure (known as morphology), the word "tenth" is made up of one linguistic form indicating a number and another form indicating ordinality. The rule governing the combination of these forms ensures that the ordinality marker "th" follows the number "ten". On the level of sound structure (known as phonology), structural analysis shows that the "n" sound in "tenth" is made differently from the "n" sound in "ten" spoken alone. Although most speakers of English are consciously aware of the rules governing internal structure of the word pieces of "tenth", they are less often aware of the rule governing its sound structure. Linguists focused on structure find and analyze rules such as these, which govern how native speakers use language.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (February 2026)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Grammar">Grammar</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Grammar"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>Grammar is a system of rules which governs the production and use of utterances in a given language. These rules apply to sound<sup id="cite&#95;ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>29<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> as well as meaning, and include componential subsets of rules, such as those pertaining to <a href="/wiki/Phonology" title="Phonology">phonology</a> (the organization of phonetic sound systems), <a href="/wiki/Morphology_(linguistics)" title="Morphology (linguistics)">morphology</a> (the formation and composition of words), and <a href="/wiki/Syntax" title="Syntax">syntax</a> (the formation and composition of phrases and sentences).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-akmajian&#95;4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-akmajian-4"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>4<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Modern <a href="/wiki/Grammar#Theoretical_frameworks" title="Grammar">frameworks that deal with the principles of grammar</a> include <a href="/wiki/Structural_linguistics" title="Structural linguistics">structural</a> and <a href="/wiki/Functional_linguistics" title="Functional linguistics">functional linguistics</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Generative_linguistics" class="mw-redirect" title="Generative linguistics">generative linguistics</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>30<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Sub-fields that focus on a grammatical study of language include the following:
</p>
<ul><li><b><a href="/wiki/Phonetics" title="Phonetics">Phonetics</a></b>, the study of the physical properties of speech sound production and perception, and delves into their acoustic and <a href="/wiki/Articulatory_phonetics" title="Articulatory phonetics">articulatory</a> properties</li>
<li><b><a href="/wiki/Phonology" title="Phonology">Phonology</a></b>, the study of sounds as abstract elements in the speaker's mind that distinguish meaning (<a href="/wiki/Phonemes" class="mw-redirect" title="Phonemes">phonemes</a>)</li>
<li><b><a href="/wiki/Morphology_(linguistics)" title="Morphology (linguistics)">Morphology</a></b>, the study of morphemes, or the internal structures of words and how they can be modified</li>
<li><b><a href="/wiki/Syntax" title="Syntax">Syntax</a></b>, the study of how words combine to form grammatical phrases and <a href="/wiki/Sentence_(linguistics)" title="Sentence (linguistics)">sentences</a></li>
<li><b><a href="/wiki/Semantics" title="Semantics">Semantics</a></b>, the study of lexical and grammatical aspects of meaning<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Meaning&#95;and&#95;Grammar:&#95;An&#95;Introductio&#95;31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Meaning_and_Grammar:_An_Introductio-31"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>31<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li><b><a href="/wiki/Pragmatics" title="Pragmatics">Pragmatics</a></b>, the study of how <a href="/wiki/Utterance" title="Utterance">utterances</a> are used in <a href="/wiki/Speech_acts" class="mw-redirect" title="Speech acts">communicative acts</a>, and the role played by situational context and non-linguistic knowledge in the transmission of meaning<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Meaning&#95;and&#95;Grammar:&#95;An&#95;Introductio&#95;31-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Meaning_and_Grammar:_An_Introductio-31"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>31<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li><b><a href="/wiki/Discourse_analysis" title="Discourse analysis">Discourse analysis</a></b>, the analysis of language use in <a href="/wiki/Text_(literary_theory)" title="Text (literary theory)">texts</a> (spoken, written, or signed)</li>
<li><b><a href="/wiki/Stylistics_(field_of_study)" class="mw-redirect" title="Stylistics (field of study)">Stylistics</a></b>, the study of linguistic factors (rhetoric, diction, stress) that place a discourse in context</li>
<li><b><a href="/wiki/Semiotics" title="Semiotics">Semiotics</a></b>, the study of signs and sign processes (semiosis), indication, designation, likeness, analogy, metaphor, symbolism, signification, and communication</li></ul>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Discourse">Discourse</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Discourse"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>Discourse is language as social practice (Baynham, 1995) and is a multilayered concept. As a social practice, discourse embodies different ideologies through written and spoken texts. Discourse analysis can examine or expose these ideologies. Discourse not only influences genre, which is selected based on specific contexts but also, at a micro level, shapes language as text (spoken or written) down to the phonological and lexico-grammatical levels. Grammar and discourse are linked as parts of a system.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>32<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> A particular discourse becomes a language variety when it is used in this way for a particular purpose, and is referred to as a <a href="/wiki/Register_(sociolinguistics)" title="Register (sociolinguistics)">register</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>33<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> There may be certain lexical additions (new words) that are brought into play because of the expertise of the community of people within a certain domain of specialization. Thus, registers and discourses distinguish themselves not only through specialized vocabulary but also, in some cases, through distinct stylistic choices. People in the medical fraternity, for example, may use some medical terminology in their communication that is specialized to the field of medicine. This is often referred to as being part of the "medical discourse", and so on.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Lexicon">Lexicon</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Lexicon"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>The lexicon is a catalogue of words and terms that are stored in a speaker's mind. The lexicon consists of words and <a href="/wiki/Bound_morphemes" class="mw-redirect" title="Bound morphemes">bound morphemes</a>, which are parts of words that can not stand alone, like <a href="/wiki/Affixes" class="mw-redirect" title="Affixes">affixes</a>. In some analyses, compound words and certain classes of idiomatic expressions and other collocations are also considered to be part of the lexicon. Dictionaries represent attempts at listing, in alphabetical order, the lexicon of a given language; usually, however, bound morphemes are not included. <a href="/wiki/Lexicography" title="Lexicography">Lexicography</a>, closely linked with the domain of semantics, is the science of mapping the words into an encyclopedia or a dictionary. The creation and addition of new words (into the lexicon) is called coining or <a href="/wiki/Neologization" class="mw-redirect" title="Neologization">neologization</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>34<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and the new words are called <a href="/wiki/Neologism" title="Neologism">neologisms</a>.
</p><p>It is often believed that a speaker's capacity for language lies in the quantity of words stored in the lexicon. However, this is often considered a myth by linguists. The capacity for the use of language is considered by many linguists to lie primarily in the domain of grammar, and to be linked with <a href="/wiki/Linguistic_competence" title="Linguistic competence">competence</a>, rather than with the growth of vocabulary. Even a very small lexicon is theoretically capable of producing an infinite number of sentences.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Vocabulary" title="Vocabulary">Vocabulary</a> size is relevant as a measure of comprehension. There is general consensus that reading comprehension of a written text in English requires 98% coverage, meaning that the person understands 98% of the words in the text.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>35<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The question of how much vocabulary is needed is therefore related to which texts or conversations need to be understood. A common estimate is 6-7,000 <a href="/wiki/Word_family" title="Word family">word families</a> to understand a wide range of conversations and 8-9,000 word families to be able to read a wide range of written texts.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>36<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Style">Style</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Style"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Stylistics_(field_of_study)" class="mw-redirect" title="Stylistics (field of study)">Stylistics</a> also involves the study of written, signed, or spoken discourse through varying speech communities, genres, and editorial or narrative formats in the mass media.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>37<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> It involves the study and interpretation of texts for aspects of their linguistic and tonal style. Stylistic analysis entails the analysis of description of particular <a href="/wiki/Dialects" class="mw-redirect" title="Dialects">dialects</a> and <a href="/wiki/Register_(sociolinguistics)" title="Register (sociolinguistics)">registers</a> used by speech communities. Stylistic features include <a href="/wiki/Rhetoric" title="Rhetoric">rhetoric</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>38<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> diction, stress, satire, <a href="/wiki/Irony" title="Irony">irony</a>, dialogue, and other forms of phonetic variations. Stylistic analysis can also include the study of language in canonical works of literature, popular fiction, news, advertisements, and other forms of communication in popular culture as well. It is usually seen as a variation in communication that changes from speaker to speaker and community to community. In short, Stylistics is the interpretation of text.
</p><p>In the 1960s, <a href="/wiki/Jacques_Derrida" title="Jacques Derrida">Jacques Derrida</a>, for instance, further distinguished between speech and writing, by proposing that written language be studied as a linguistic medium of communication in itself.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>39<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Palaeography" title="Palaeography">Palaeography</a> is therefore the discipline that studies the evolution of written scripts (as signs and symbols) in language.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>40<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The formal study of language also led to the growth of fields like <a href="/wiki/Psycholinguistics" title="Psycholinguistics">psycholinguistics</a>, which explores the representation and function of language in the mind; <a href="/wiki/Neurolinguistics" title="Neurolinguistics">neurolinguistics</a>, which studies language processing in the brain; <a href="/wiki/Biolinguistics" title="Biolinguistics">biolinguistics</a>, which studies the biology and evolution of language; and <a href="/wiki/Language_acquisition" title="Language acquisition">language acquisition</a>, which investigates how children and adults acquire the knowledge of one or more languages.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Methodology">Methodology</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Methodology"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1305433154" /><table class="box-More&#95;citations&#95;needed&#95;section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/60px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/120px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Linguistics" title="Special:EditPage/Linguistics">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>&#32;in this section. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">February 2024</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Modern linguistics is primarily <a href="/wiki/Descriptive_linguistics" class="mw-redirect" title="Descriptive linguistics">descriptive</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Martinet&#95;41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martinet-41"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>41<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Linguists describe and explain features of language without making subjective judgments on whether a particular feature or usage is "good" or "bad". This is analogous to practice in other sciences: a <a href="/wiki/Zoologist" class="mw-redirect" title="Zoologist">zoologist</a> studies the animal kingdom without making subjective judgments on whether a particular species is "better" or "worse" than another.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Linguistic_prescription" title="Linguistic prescription">Prescription</a>, on the other hand, is an attempt to promote particular linguistic usages over others, often favoring a particular dialect or "<a href="/wiki/Acrolect" class="mw-redirect" title="Acrolect">acrolect</a>". This may have the aim of establishing a <a href="/wiki/Standard_language" title="Standard language">linguistic standard</a>, which can aid communication over large geographical areas. It may also, however, be an attempt by speakers of one language or dialect to exert influence over speakers of other languages or dialects (see <a href="/wiki/Linguistic_imperialism" title="Linguistic imperialism">Linguistic imperialism</a>). An extreme version of prescriptivism can be found among <a href="/wiki/Censorship" title="Censorship">censors</a>, who attempt to eradicate words and structures that they consider to be destructive to society. Prescription, however, may be practised appropriately in <a href="/wiki/Language_education" title="Language education">language instruction</a>, like in <a href="/wiki/English_language_teaching" class="mw-redirect" title="English language teaching">ELT</a>, where certain fundamental grammatical rules and lexical items need to be introduced to a second-language speaker who is attempting to <a href="/wiki/Language_acquisition" title="Language acquisition">acquire</a> the language.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Sources">Sources</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Sources"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>Most contemporary linguists work under the assumption that <a href="/wiki/Spoken_language" title="Spoken language">spoken data</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sign_language" title="Sign language">signed data</a> are more fundamental than <a href="/wiki/Written_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Written language">written data</a>. This is because
</p>
<ul><li>Speech appears to be universal to all human beings capable of producing and perceiving it, while there have been many cultures and speech communities that lack written communication;</li>
<li>Features appear in speech which are not always recorded in writing, including <a href="/wiki/Phonological_rule" title="Phonological rule">phonological rules</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sound_change" title="Sound change">sound changes</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Speech_error" title="Speech error">speech errors</a>;</li>
<li>All natural writing systems reflect a spoken language (or potentially a signed one), even with <a href="/wiki/Pictographic" class="mw-redirect" title="Pictographic">pictographic</a> scripts like <a href="/wiki/Dongba" title="Dongba">Dongba</a> writing <a href="/wiki/Naxi_language" title="Naxi language">Naxi</a> <a href="/wiki/Homophone" title="Homophone">homophones</a> with the same pictogram, and text in writing systems used for two languages changing to fit the spoken language being recorded;</li>
<li>Speech evolved before human beings invented writing;</li>
<li>Individuals learn to speak and process spoken language more easily and earlier than they do with writing.</li></ul>
<p>Nonetheless, linguists agree that the study of written language can be worthwhile and valuable. For research that relies on <a href="/wiki/Corpus_linguistics" title="Corpus linguistics">corpus linguistics</a> and <a href="/wiki/Computational_linguistics" title="Computational linguistics">computational linguistics</a>, written language is often much more convenient for processing large amounts of linguistic data. Large corpora of spoken language are difficult to create and hard to find, and are typically <a href="/wiki/Transcription_(linguistics)" title="Transcription (linguistics)">transcribed</a> and written. In addition, linguists have turned to text-based discourse occurring in various formats of <a href="/wiki/Computer-mediated_communication" title="Computer-mediated communication">computer-mediated communication</a> as a viable site for linguistic inquiry.
</p><p>The study of writing systems themselves, graphemics, is, in any case, considered a branch of linguistics.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Analysis">Analysis</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Analysis"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>Before the 20th century, linguists analysed language on a <a href="/wiki/Diachronic_linguistics" class="mw-redirect" title="Diachronic linguistics">diachronic</a> plane, which was historical in focus. This meant that they would compare linguistic features and try to analyse language from the point of view of how it had changed between then and later. However, with the rise of Saussurean linguistics in the 20th century, the focus shifted to a more <a href="/wiki/Synchronic_linguistics" class="mw-redirect" title="Synchronic linguistics">synchronic</a> approach, where the study was geared towards analysis and comparison between different language variations, which existed at the same given point of time.
</p><p>At another level, the <a href="/wiki/Syntagmatic_analysis" title="Syntagmatic analysis">syntagmatic</a> plane of linguistic analysis entails the comparison between the way words are sequenced, within the syntax of a sentence. For example, the article "the" is followed by a noun, because of the syntagmatic relation between the words. The <a href="/wiki/Paradigmatic_analysis" title="Paradigmatic analysis">paradigmatic</a> plane, on the other hand, focuses on an analysis that is based on the paradigms or concepts that are embedded in a given text. In this case, words of the same type or class may be replaced in the text with each other to achieve the same conceptual understanding.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="History">History</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: History"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_linguistics" title="History of linguistics">History of linguistics</a></div>
<p>The earliest activities in the <a href="/wiki/Linguistic_description" title="Linguistic description">description of language</a> have been attributed to the 6th-century&#160;BC Indian grammarian <a href="/wiki/P%C4%81%E1%B9%87ini" title="Pāṇini">Pāṇini</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>42<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>43<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> who composed a <a href="/wiki/Formal_grammar" title="Formal grammar">formal description</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Sanskrit" title="Sanskrit">Sanskrit language</a> in his <span title="International Alphabet of Sanskrit transliteration"><i lang="sa-Latn"><a href="/wiki/A%E1%B9%A3%E1%B9%AD%C4%81dhy%C4%81y%C4%AB" title="Aṣṭādhyāyī">Aṣṭādhyāyī</a></i></span>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>44<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>45<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Today, modern-day theories on <a href="/wiki/Generative_grammar" title="Generative grammar">grammar</a> employ many of the principles that were laid down then.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>46<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Nomenclature">Nomenclature</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Nomenclature"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>Before the 20th century, the term <i><a href="/wiki/Philology" title="Philology">philology</a></i>, first attested in 1716,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Etymonline&#95;Definition&#95;of&#95;Philology&#95;47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Etymonline_Definition_of_Philology-47"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>47<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> was commonly used to refer to the study of language, which was then predominantly historical in focus.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Nichols&#95;65-1&#95;48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nichols_65-1-48"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>48<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Understanding&#95;49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Understanding-49"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>49<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Since <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_de_Saussure" title="Ferdinand de Saussure">Ferdinand de Saussure</a>'s insistence on the importance of <a href="/wiki/Synchronic_analysis_(linguistics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Synchronic analysis (linguistics)">synchronic analysis</a>, however, this focus has shifted<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Understanding&#95;49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Understanding-49"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>49<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and the term <i>philology</i> is now generally used for the "study of a language's grammar, history, and literary tradition", especially in the United States<sup id="cite&#95;ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>50<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> (where philology has never been very popularly considered as the "science of language").<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Etymonline&#95;Definition&#95;of&#95;Philology&#95;47-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Etymonline_Definition_of_Philology-47"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>47<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Although the term <i>linguist</i> in the sense of "a student of language" dates from 1641,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-etymonline&#95;linguist&#95;51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-etymonline_linguist-51"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>51<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> the term <i>linguistics</i> is first attested in 1847.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-etymonline&#95;linguist&#95;51-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-etymonline_linguist-51"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>51<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> It is now the usual term in English for the scientific study of language,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>52<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>53<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> though <i>linguistic science</i> is sometimes used.
</p><p>Linguistics is a <a href="/wiki/Interdisciplinarity" title="Interdisciplinarity">multi-disciplinary</a> field of research that combines tools from natural sciences, social sciences, <a href="/wiki/Formal_science" title="Formal science">formal sciences</a>, and the humanities.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>55<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>56<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>57<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Many linguists, such as David Crystal, conceptualize the field as being primarily scientific.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>58<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The term <i>linguist</i> applies to someone who studies language or is a researcher within the field, or to someone who uses the tools of the discipline to describe and analyse specific languages.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-American&#95;Heritage&#95;2000&#95;59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-American_Heritage_2000-59"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>59<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Early_grammarians">Early grammarians</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Early grammarians"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Philology" title="Philology">Philology</a> and <a href="/wiki/Grammarian_(Greco-Roman)" title="Grammarian (Greco-Roman)">Grammarian (Greco-Roman)</a></div>
<p>An early formal study of language was undertaken in India by the 6th-century&#160;BC grammarian <a href="/wiki/P%C4%81%E1%B9%87ini" title="Pāṇini">Pāṇini</a>, who formulated 3,959 rules of Sanskrit <a href="/wiki/Morphology_(linguistics)" title="Morphology (linguistics)">morphology</a>. Pāṇini's systematic classification of the sounds of <a href="/wiki/Sanskrit" title="Sanskrit">Sanskrit</a> into consonants and vowels, and word classes, such as nouns and verbs, was the first known instance of its kind. In the Middle East, <a href="/wiki/Sibawayh" title="Sibawayh">Sibawayh</a>, a Persian, made a detailed description of Arabic in AD 760 in his monumental work, <i>Al-kitab fii an-naħw</i> (<span title="Arabic-language text"><span lang="ar" dir="rtl">الكتاب في النحو</span></span>, <i>The Book on Grammar</i>), the first known author to distinguish between <a href="/wiki/Phonetics" title="Phonetics">sounds</a> and <a href="/wiki/Phonology" title="Phonology">phonemes (sounds as units of a linguistic system)</a>. Western interest in the study of languages began somewhat later than in the East,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>60<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> but the grammarians of the classical languages did not use the same methods or reach the same conclusions as their contemporaries in the Indic world. Early interest in language in the West was a part of philosophy, not of grammatical description. The first insights into semantic theory were made by <a href="/wiki/Plato" title="Plato">Plato</a> in his <a href="/wiki/Cratylus_(dialogue)" title="Cratylus (dialogue)"><i>Cratylus</i> dialogue</a>, where he argues that words denote concepts that are eternal and exist in the world of ideas. This work is the first to use the word etymology to describe the history of a word's meaning. Around 280 BC, one of <a href="/wiki/Alexander_the_Great" title="Alexander the Great">Alexander the Great</a>'s successors founded a university (see <a href="/wiki/Musaeum" class="mw-redirect" title="Musaeum">Musaeum</a>) in <a href="/wiki/Alexandria" title="Alexandria">Alexandria</a>, where a school of philologists studied the ancient texts in Greek, and taught Greek to speakers of other languages. While this school was the first to use the word "grammar" in its modern sense, Plato had used the word in its original meaning as "<a href="/wiki/Art_of_Grammar" class="mw-redirect" title="Art of Grammar">téchnē grammatikḗ</a>" (<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1301836668">.mw-parser-output .polytonic{font-family:"SBL Greek","SBL BibLit","New Athena Unicode","EB Garamond","EB Garamond 12","Foulis Greek","Garamond Libre","Noto Sans","Noto Serif","Cardo","Gentium Plus","Gentium Book Plus","Garamond","Palatino Linotype","DejaVu Sans","DejaVu Serif","FreeSerif","FreeSans","Arial Unicode MS","Lucida Sans Unicode","Lucida Grande","Code2000",sans-serif}</style><span title="Polytonic Greek" class="polytonic">Τέχνη Γραμματική</span>), the "art of writing", which is also the title of one of the most important works of the Alexandrine school by <a href="/wiki/Dionysius_Thrax" title="Dionysius Thrax">Dionysius Thrax</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>61<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Throughout the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a>, the study of language was subsumed under the topic of philology, the study of ancient languages and texts, practised by such educators as <a href="/wiki/Roger_Ascham" title="Roger Ascham">Roger Ascham</a>, <a href="/wiki/Wolfgang_Ratke" title="Wolfgang Ratke">Wolfgang Ratke</a>, and <a href="/wiki/John_Amos_Comenius" title="John Amos Comenius">John Amos Comenius</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>62<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Comparative_philology">Comparative philology</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Comparative philology"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>In the 18th century, the first use of the <a href="/wiki/Comparative_method" title="Comparative method">comparative method</a> by <a href="/wiki/William_Jones_(philologist)" title="William Jones (philologist)">William Jones</a> sparked the rise of <a href="/wiki/Comparative_linguistics" title="Comparative linguistics">comparative linguistics</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>63<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Bloomfield attributes "the first great scientific linguistic work of the world" to <a href="/wiki/Jacob_Grimm" title="Jacob Grimm">Jacob Grimm</a>, who wrote <i>Deutsche Grammatik</i>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Bloomfield&#95;1914&#95;311&#95;64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bloomfield_1914_311-64"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>64<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> It was soon followed by other authors writing similar comparative studies on other language groups of Europe. The study of language was broadened from <a href="/wiki/Indo-European_languages" title="Indo-European languages">Indo-European</a> to language in general by <a href="/wiki/Wilhelm_von_Humboldt" title="Wilhelm von Humboldt">Wilhelm von Humboldt</a>, of whom Bloomfield asserts:<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Bloomfield&#95;1914&#95;311&#95;64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bloomfield_1914_311-64"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>64<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1244412712">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 32px}.mw-parser-output .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;margin-top:0}@media(min-width:500px){.mw-parser-output .templatequotecite{padding-left:1.6em}}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>This study received its foundation at the hands of the Prussian statesman and scholar Wilhelm von Humboldt (1767–1835), especially in the first volume of his work on Kavi, the literary language of Java, entitled <i><span title="German-language text"><i lang="de">Über die Verschiedenheit des menschlichen Sprachbaues und ihren Einfluß auf die geistige Entwickelung des Menschengeschlechts</i></span></i> (<i>On the Variety of the Structure of Human Language and its Influence upon the Mental Development of the Human Race</i>).</p></blockquote>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="20th-century_developments">20th-century developments</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: 20th-century developments"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>There was a shift of focus from historical and comparative linguistics to synchronic analysis in early 20th century. Structural analysis was improved by <a href="/wiki/Leonard_Bloomfield" title="Leonard Bloomfield">Leonard Bloomfield</a>, <a href="/wiki/Louis_Hjelmslev" title="Louis Hjelmslev">Louis Hjelmslev</a>; and <a href="/wiki/Zellig_Harris" title="Zellig Harris">Zellig Harris</a> who also developed methods of <a href="/wiki/Discourse_analysis" title="Discourse analysis">discourse analysis</a>. Functional analysis was developed by the <a href="/wiki/Prague_linguistic_circle" title="Prague linguistic circle">Prague linguistic circle</a> and <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Martinet" title="André Martinet">André Martinet</a>. As sound recording devices became commonplace in the 1960s, dialectal recordings were made and archived, and the <a href="/wiki/Audio-lingual_method" title="Audio-lingual method">audio-lingual method</a> provided a technological solution to foreign language learning. The 1960s also saw a new rise of comparative linguistics: the study of <a href="/wiki/Language_universals" class="mw-redirect" title="Language universals">language universals</a> in <a href="/wiki/Linguistic_typology" title="Linguistic typology">linguistic typology</a>. Towards the end of the century the field of linguistics became divided into further areas of interest with the advent of <a href="/wiki/Language_technology" title="Language technology">language technology</a> and digitalized <a href="/wiki/Corpus_linguistics" title="Corpus linguistics">corpora</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>65<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>66<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>67<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Areas_of_research">Areas of research</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Areas of research"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1305433154" /><table class="box-More&#95;citations&#95;needed plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/60px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/120px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Linguistics" title="Special:EditPage/Linguistics">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>&#32;in this section. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.<br /><small><span class="plainlinks"><i>Find sources:</i>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?as_eq=wikipedia&amp;q=%22Linguistics%22">"Linguistics"</a>&#160;–&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?tbm=nws&amp;q=%22Linguistics%22+-wikipedia&amp;tbs=ar:1">news</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?&amp;q=%22Linguistics%22&amp;tbs=bkt:s&amp;tbm=bks">newspapers</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?tbs=bks:1&amp;q=%22Linguistics%22+-wikipedia">books</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=%22Linguistics%22">scholar</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.jstor.org/action/doBasicSearch?Query=%22Linguistics%22&amp;acc=on&amp;wc=on">JSTOR</a></span></small></span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">August 2021</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Sociolinguistics">Sociolinguistics</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Sociolinguistics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Sociolinguistics" title="Sociolinguistics">Sociolinguistics</a></div>
<p>Sociolinguistics is the study of how language is shaped by social factors. This sub-discipline focuses on the synchronic approach of linguistics, and looks at how a language in general, or a set of languages, display variation and varieties at a given point in time. The study of language variation and the different varieties of language through dialects, registers, and idiolects can be tackled through a study of style, as well as through analysis of discourse. Sociolinguists research both style and discourse in language, as well as the theoretical factors that are at play between language and society.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Developmental_linguistics">Developmental linguistics</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Developmental linguistics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Developmental_linguistics" title="Developmental linguistics">Developmental linguistics</a></div>
<p>Developmental linguistics is the study of the development of linguistic ability in individuals, particularly <a href="/wiki/Language_acquisition" title="Language acquisition">the acquisition of language</a> in childhood. Some of the questions that developmental linguistics looks into are how children acquire different languages, how adults can acquire a second language, and what the process of language acquisition is.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>68<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Neurolinguistics">Neurolinguistics</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Neurolinguistics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Neurolinguistics" title="Neurolinguistics">Neurolinguistics</a></div>
<p>Neurolinguistics is the study of the structures in the human brain that underlie grammar and communication. Researchers are drawn to the field from a variety of backgrounds, bringing along a variety of experimental techniques as well as widely varying theoretical perspectives. Much work in neurolinguistics is informed by models in <a href="/wiki/Psycholinguistics" title="Psycholinguistics">psycholinguistics</a> and <a href="/wiki/Theoretical_linguistics" title="Theoretical linguistics">theoretical linguistics</a>, and is focused on investigating how the brain can implement the processes that theoretical and psycholinguistics propose are necessary in producing and comprehending language. Neurolinguists study the physiological mechanisms by which the brain processes information related to language, and evaluate linguistic and psycholinguistic theories, using <a href="/wiki/Aphasiology" title="Aphasiology">aphasiology</a>, <a href="/wiki/Brain_imaging" class="mw-redirect" title="Brain imaging">brain imaging</a>, electrophysiology, and computer modelling. Amongst the structures of the brain involved in the mechanisms of neurolinguistics, the cerebellum which contains the highest numbers of neurons has a major role in terms of predictions required to produce language.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>69<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Applied_linguistics">Applied linguistics</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Linguistics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Applied linguistics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Applied_linguistics" title="Applied linguistics">Applied linguistics</a></div>
